```python
    # State 1: at I
    # State 2: at M
    # State 3: at A
    
    # When we return to D from A, that completes one DIMA sequence
    
    # We can use memoization: memo[r][c][state] = max path length from this state
    # But we also need to detect cycles
    
    max_cycles = 0
    has_infinite = False
    
    # For cycle detection: if we can go from D to I to M to A and back to D, that's a cycle
    # So we need to find if there's a path D->I->M->A->D
    
    # Create a function to find path from a given (r,c) with current state
    memo = {}
    
    def dfs(r, c, state, visited_d_cells=None):
        nonlocal max_cycles, has_infinite
        
        # State: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # If we return to D state, we've completed one DIMA sequence
        # But we're in state 0, so completing one sequence means going from A to D
        # Actually, we complete a sequence when we go from A to D
        
        # We need to track which D cells we've visited to avoid infinite loops
        if visited_d_cells is None:
            visited_d_cells = set()
        
        # Current state is at position (r,c) with state
        # The key to detect infinite is if we can return to a D cell we've already visited
        # But we complete a sequence when we return to D
        
        # Actually, we should track the current path and see if we can form a loop
        # But simpler: if we can go from D to I to M to A to D, we have cycle
        
        # We need to return the maximum number of cycles we can achieve from this state
        
        # Let's try to find the maximum path length from this state
        key = (r, c, state, tuple(sorted(visited_d_cells)))
        
        if key in memo:
            return memo[key]
        
        current_char = grid[r][c]
        
        # Check if current char matches expected state
        expected_char = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][state]
        if current_char != expected_char:
            memo[key] = 0
            return 0
        
        # If we're at D (state 0), we need to go to I
        # If we're at I (state 1), we need to go to M
        # If we're at M (state 2), we need to go to A
        # If we're at A (state 3), we need to go to D
        
        # The next state
        next_state = (state + 1) % 4

        # But we're at A, so we need to transition to D
        
        # From any state, we can go to adjacent cells with the next letter

            if is_valid(nr, nc) and grid[nr][nc] == ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A'][next_state]:

                # Check if we can continue from there
                current_view = visited_d_cells.copy()

                # But when we move to a new D cell, we need to track it

        # I'm overcomplicating. Let me use a different approach.

    # But when we are at state 3 (A), and we move to a D cell, we complete one cycle, and then we can continue from that D cell
    
    # So when we go from A to D, we complete one cycle, and then we add 1 to the cycles and continue from the new D cell
    
    # We can use memoization and the state includes the current position and current letter in the sequence
    
    # However, we need to detect if there's an infinite path, which happens if we can loop back to a D cell we've already seen, or if we can complete a cycle and repeat
    
    # Since the path is infinite only if we can form a cycle that doesn't end, and we can only form a cycle if we can return to a D cell after going I-M-A
    
    # Actually, if from any starting D, we can go to I, M, A, and then back to any D (including the start), then we have a cycle
    
    # So we can check: is there a path that goes from a D cell to I to M to A and then back to a D cell?
    
    # We can do this with BFS from each D cell, trying to reach a D cell after going to I, M, A
    
    # But we need to do it for all D cells to see if any such path exists
    
    # Let me implement a BFS to see if there's a cycle
    
    # We'll try to find a path: D -> I -> M -> A -> D
    # We'll try for each starting D cell, see if we can reach another D cell after going through I,M,A
    
    # If we can, then we have an infinite path
    
    # Also, we need to find the maximum path length from each starting D cell
    
    # First, check for cycle
    
    from collections import deque
    
    cycle_found = False
    
    # For each D cell, try to reach another D cell by going D->I->M->A->D
    for start_r, start_c in d_cells:
        # BFS from start_r, start_c in state 0 (D)
        # We want to reach any D cell in state 0 after going to I,M,A
        queue = deque()
        # (r, c, state, visited_d_cells) - but visited_d_cells is not needed
        
        # We'll use visited states to detect cycles
        # visited[r][c][state] = True if we've been here
        visited = {}
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0))
        visited[(start_r, start_c, 0)] = True
        
        # We want to see if we can reach any D cell after going through I,M,A
        # So when we are at state 3 (A), and we move to a D cell, we complete a cycle if that D cell is different from start?
        # Actually, if we move to any D cell, we complete a cycle, and if we can do this again, we have infinite
        
        # But we only need to know if there's any such path to detect infinite
        
        # Process BFS
        found_cycle = False
        while queue:
            r, c, state = queue.popleft()
            
            # Current cell has letter should match state
            if grid[r][c] != ['D','I','M','A'][state]:
                continue
                
            # Next state
            next_state = (state + 1) % 4
            
            # Try to move to adjacent cells with next letter
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if not is_valid(nr, nc):
                    continue
                    
                if grid[nr][nc] == ['D','I','M','A'][next_state]:
                    new_state = next_state
                    
                    # If we are at state 3 (A), and we move to a D cell, we complete a DIMA cycle
                    # This completion means we can start over from that D cell
                    if new_state == 3:  # We are at A, next will be D
                        # We are moving to a D cell from A, so we are completing a cycle
                        # Check if this new D cell is different from the start cell
                        # We can complete one cycle and then continue from there
                        # So if we reach any D cell from A (after going through I,M), we have a cycle
                        # Because then we can repeat
                        if (nr, nc) in d_cells and (nr, nc) != (start_r, start_c):
                            # We found a cycle from start to (nr,nc)
                            cycle_found = True
                            break
                        # If we reach start cell itself, we also have a cycle (self-loop)
                        if (nr, nc) == (start_r, start_c):
                            cycle_found = True
                            break
                            
                    # If we haven't completed a cycle yet, we just continue
                    key = (nr, nc, new_state)
                    if key not in visited:
                        visited[key] = True
                        queue.append((nr, nc, new_state))
            
            if cycle_found:
                break
                
        if cycle_found:
            has_infinite = True
            break
    
    if has_infinite:
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # If no cycle, find maximum number of DIMA sequences from each D cell
    # Use DFS with dynamic programming
    
    # dp[r][c][state] = maximum number of complete cycles we can form starting from (r,c) in state
    # When we go from A to D, we complete one cycle, and then we can continue from that D cell
    
    # But since no cycle, we won't loop back, so we can just find the path length
    
    # For each D cell, we want to find how many complete DIMA sequences we can form
    
    # We'll do DFS from each D cell with state 0
    
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # For each D cell, do DFS to find max cycles
    for start_r, start_c in d_cells:
        # We'll use memoization for state (r, c, state)
        memo = {}
        
        def dfs(r, c, state):
            if (r, c, state) in memo:
                return memo[(r, c, state)]
            
            # Current state should match cell letter
            if grid[r][c] != ['D','I','M','A'][state]:
                memo[(r, c, state)] = 0
                return 0
            
            # Next state
            next_state = (state + 1) % 4
            
            # If next_state is 0 (D), that means we completed a DIMA cycle (D->I->M->A->D)
            # When we move to a D cell, we complete one cycle
            # So we add 1 to the cycle count and continue from that D cell
            
            # But we need to make sure we don't go to a D cell that would create a cycle
            # Since we already checked no cycle exists, we won't have cycles, so we just continue
            
            max_cycles_here = 0
            
            # Try to move to adjacent cells with next letter
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if not is_valid(nr, nc):
                    continue
                    
                if grid[nr][nc] == ['D','I','M','A'][next_state]:
                    # Move to (nr, nc) with next_state
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, next_state)

                        # When we go from A to D, we complete one cycle

    def dfs(r, c, state, current_cycles):

    # Let me define a new DFS that returns the maximum number of complete cycles achievable from (r,c) in current state

    def dfs(r, c, state):

# But I see a flaw: in the cycle detection, I only check if we can go from D to I to M to A to a D, but that might not capture all cycles
    
# Also, in the DFS for cycle detection, I only check when we go from A to a D cell, and if that D cell is different from start or is start, we have a cycle
    
# Let me fix the cycle detection to be more comprehensive

# Actually, I think the cycle detection should be: if we can find any path that goes from a D cell to I to M to A and then to any D cell, then we have a cycle
    
# That should be sufficient because once we have that path, we can repeat it infinitely
    
# Also, in the DFS for no cycle, we need to be sure that we don't have any such path

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    def is_valid(r, c):
        return 0 <= r < n and 0 <= c < m
    
    # Find all D cells
    d_cells = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_cells.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_cells:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Check for cycle: can we go from a D to I to M to A and back to a D?
    has_cycle = False
    from collections import deque
    
    # For each D cell, try to see if we can reach a D cell after going through I,M,A
    for start_r, start_c in d_cells:
        # BFS to see if we can reach any D cell from (start_r, start_c) by going D->I->M->A->D
        # State: (r, c, state) where state is 0,1,2,3 for D,I,M,A
        # We want to know if we can reach any (r, c) with state 0 (D) after state 3 (A)
        visited = set()
        queue = deque()
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0))
        visited.add((start_r, start_c, 0))
        
        found_cycle = False
        while queue:
            r, c, state = queue.popleft()
            
            # Current cell should be correct
            if grid[r][c] != ['D','I','M','A'][state]:
                continue
                
            # Next state
            next_state = (state + 1) % 4
            
            # Try to move to adjacent cells
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if not is_valid(nr, nc):
                    continue
                    
                # Check if next cell has the required letter
                if grid[nr][nc] == ['D','I','M','A'][next_state]:
                    new_state = next_state
                    key = (nr, nc, new_state)
                    
                    if new_state == 3:  # We are at A, next will be D
                        # If we can move to a D cell from A, we have completed a cycle
                        # The completion is when we go from A to D, which is when we move from state 3 to state 0
                        # So we are at A, and we will move to a D cell
                        # We need to see if that D cell is different from the start or the same
                        # But we are at A, so we are not at D yet
                        # When we move from (nr, nc) which is at state 3, to a D cell, that completes a cycle
                        # So we can only complete that cycle if we move to a D cell
                        # But in this BFS, we are only at state 3, not at state 0
                        # So we need to check if after moving to a cell with state 0 (D), we complete a cycle
                        
                        # We are at (nr, nc) with state 3, so we can move to a D cell from here
                        # The move to a D cell will complete a cycle
                        # So if there exists a D cell from which we can move, then we have a cycle
                        # But we are already in the path, so if we can move from (nr, nc) to a D cell, that means we have a cycle
                        # However, we are not at that D cell yet
                        # We should simulate the move to a D cell because that completes a cycle
                        
                        # To detect a cycle, we need to see if after going to A, we can move to any D cell
                        # So if (nr, nc) has state 3, and we can move to a D cell, then we have a cycle
                        # We can do this by checking if from (nr, nc) we can move to any D cell in the next step
                        # But in this BFS, we are only at state 3
                        
                        # We need to know if from (nr, nc) we can move to a D cell
                        # We can check this by looking at adjacent cells with letter 'D'
                        for dr2, dc2 in directions:
                            nr2, nc2 = nr + dr2, nc + dc2
                            if is_valid(nr2, nc2) and grid[nr2][nc2] == 'D':
                                # We can move to a D cell from (nr, nc) at state 3
                                # This completes a cycle
                                # So we have a cycle
                                found_cycle = True
                                break
                        if found_cycle:
                            break
                    else:
                        # We are moving to state 1,2, or 3 (not A)
                        if key not in visited:
                            visited.add(key)
                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_state))
            
            if found_cycle:
                break
        
        if found_cycle:
            has_cycle = True
            break
    
    if has_cycle:
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # If no cycle, find maximum number of complete DIMA sequences from each D cell
    # Use DFS with memoization to find max cycles from each state
    memo = {}
    
    def dfs(r, c, state):
        if (r, c, state) in memo:
            return memo[(r, c, state)]
        
        # Check if current cell has correct letter
        if grid[r][c] != ['D','I','M','A'][state]:
            memo[(r, c, state)] = 0
            return 0
        
        next_state = (state + 1) % 4
        max_cycles = 0
        
        for dr, dc in directions:
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if not is_valid(nr, nc):
                continue
                
            if grid[nr][nc] == ['D','I','M','A'][next_state]:
                if next_state == 3:  # We are moving to A from M
                    # We are at A in the next state, so from here we can complete a cycle when we go to a D cell
                    # But we can only complete when we go to a D cell, so we need to see what happens after
                    # We will move to (nr, nc) with state 3
                    # Then from there, we can go to a D cell, which will complete a cycle
                    # But in the DFS, when we go from state 3 to state 0, we complete one cycle
                    # So we need to calculate the cycles from that path
                    # The cycles from (nr, nc) in state 3 is not directly given, but we can call dfs on that state
                    # But in our DFS, we haven't defined that
                    
                    # We need to modify: when we move to state 3, we will then eventually move to a D cell and complete a cycle
                    # So the number of cycles from here is 1 (from A to D) plus the cycles from the new D cell
                    # But we are at state 3, so we need to call dfs on (nr, nc) with state 3
                    # Then from there, when it goes to a D cell, it will complete one cycle
                    
                    # Actually, the function dfs(r, c, state) should return the number of complete cycles we can achieve from this state
                    # When we are at state 3, we can move to a D cell, and that moves us to state 0, completing one cycle
                    # So we should have a path that completes one cycle and then continues
                    # But in our current design, we haven't captured that
                    
                    # Let me change the DFS to return the number of cycles when we complete from A to D
                    # We'll call the function on (nr, nc) with state 3, and it will return the number of cycles from there
                    
                    # But when we are at state 3, we can go to a D cell, which will complete one cycle
                    # So the total cycles from this move is 1 + (cycles from the new D cell)
                    # However, the new D cell will be in state 0, and we can from there continue
                    
                    # So we need to call dfs on (nr, nc, 3) and add 1 for the cycle we complete when we go to a D cell
                    # But when we are at state 3, we don't complete a cycle yet

                    # So the DFS for state 3 should return the number of cycles from that state
                    # And when we move to a D cell, we complete one cycle and then continue from that D cell

                    cycles_from_new = dfs(nr, nc, next_state)
                    max_cycles = max(max_cycles, cycles_from_new)

        return max_cycles
```

```python
            return max_cycles

            max_complete_cycles = max(max_complete_cycles, cycles)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 2
DI

Output
Poor Dima!

Input
2 2
MA
ID

Output
Poor Inna!

Input
5 5
DIMAD
DIMAI
DIMAM
DDMAA
AAMID

Output
4



-----Note-----

Notes to the samples:

In the first test sample, Inna cannot go through name DIMA a single time.

In the second test sample, Inna can go through the infinite number of words DIMA. For that, she should move in the clockwise direction starting from the lower right corner.

In the third test sample the best strategy is to start from the cell in the upper left corner of the table. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with letters 'D', 'I', 'M', 'A'
2. Inna wants to go through the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The path must follow the exact sequence: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. We can only move to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of complete "DIMA" sequences we can form
6. Three possible outcomes:
   - "Poor Dima!" - can't complete even one sequence
   - "Poor Inna!" - can complete infinitely many sequences
   - A number - maximum finite number of sequences

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a graph problem where we have nodes (cells) and edges between adjacent cells with the right letter sequence.
2. From any cell with 'D', we can go to adjacent cells with 'I', then to 'M', then to 'A', then back to 'D'.
3. We need to find cycles in this directed graph.
4. If there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely, we output "Poor Inna!"
5. Otherwise, we find the maximum number of complete sequences from each starting position.

## Approach

1. Build a graph where edges exist between adjacent cells that form valid transitions in "DIMA"
2. For each cell with 'D', we can start a sequence
3. Use dynamic programming or DFS with memoization to find how many complete sequences we can make from each starting position
4. Check if any cycle exists (which would allow infinite sequences)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Create a graph representation
2. For each cell with 'D', use DFS/BFS to count maximum sequences we can go through
3. Detect cycles: if we can return to a previous state in the path, we have a cycle
4. If any path contains a cycle, return "Poor Inna!"
5. Otherwise, find the maximum count across all starting positions

Actually, let me think more carefully. Since we need to go D→I→M→A→D→I→M→A..., we need to check if we can form cycles in the path.

The key insight is that if we can complete a full cycle (D→I→M→A→D), then we can loop infinitely. So we need to detect if there's a path that returns to a previous state after forming one complete "DIMA".

Let me implement a solution using DFS with cycle detection:

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

                                    queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, seq_count))

                # But we need to find the global maximum
                current_max = 0
                for state, count in best.items():
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

                    visited = set()

                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while stack:
                        r, c, phase = stack.pop()
                        if phase == 3:  # reached A
                            return True

                            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc

                                new_phase = phase + 1
                                if new_phase == 4:  # A found, we're done
```

from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # First, check if we can even complete one sequence
    def can_complete_one():
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # Try to go D -> I -> M -> A
                    visited = set()
                    stack = [(i, j, 0)]  # (row, col, phase)
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while stack:
                        r, c, phase = stack.pop()
                        if phase == 3:  # reached A
                            return True
                            
                        next_letter = "DIMA"[phase]
                        for dr, dc in directions:
                            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                                new_phase = phase + 1
                                if new_phase == 4:  # A found, we're done
                                    return True
                                new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                                if new_state not in visited:
                                    visited.add(new_state)
                                    stack.append((nr, nc, new_phase))
        return False
    
    if not can_complete_one():
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Check for infinite cycles
    def has_cycle():
        # We need to detect if we can form a loop D->I->M->A->D that doesn't end
        # This happens if from some D, we can reach a D after completing one cycle
        # but we need to check with states
        
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    # Do BFS from (i,j) to see if we can return to a D
                    visited = set()
                    stack = [(i, j, 0)]
                    visited.add((i, j, 0))
                    
                    while stack:
                        r, c, phase = stack.pop()
                        next_letter = "DIMA"[phase]
                        
                        for dr, dc in directions:
                            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                                new_phase = phase + 1
                                if new_phase == 4:  # we reached A, now we can go to D
                                    # We're at (nr, nc) with phase 0 (D), so we can continue
                                    # This state (nr, nc, 0) might be the same as a previous state
                                    if (nr, nc, 0) in visited:
                                        # We found a cycle - we can loop infinitely
                                        return True
                                    # Add to stack to continue
                                    visited.add((nr, nc, 0))
                                    stack.append((nr, nc, 0))
                                else:
                                    new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                                    if new_state not in visited:
                                        visited.add(new_state)
                                        stack.append((nr, nc, new_phase))
        return False
    
    if has_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # Find maximum number of complete sequences
    max_sequences = 0
    
    # From each D position, find maximum sequences
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                # BFS to find maximum sequences
                # State: (r, c, phase, sequences)
                # We want to maximize sequences when we complete a cycle
                
                visited = {}
                queue = deque()
                
                # Start from (i, j, 0)
                queue.append((i, j, 0, 0))
                visited[(i, j, 0)] = 0
                
                while queue:
                    r, c, phase, seq_count = queue.popleft()
                    
                    next_letter = "DIMA"[phase]
                    for dr, dc in directions:
                        nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                        if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                            new_phase = phase + 1
                            if new_phase == 4:  # completed DIMA, now at D
                                new_seq_count = seq_count + 1
                                # We can start a new cycle from (nr, nc, 0)
                                state = (nr, nc, 0)
                                if state not in visited or visited[state] < new_seq_count:
                                    visited[state] = new_seq_count
                                    queue.append((nr, nc, 0, new_seq_count))
                            else:
                                new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                                if new_state not in visited or visited[new_state] < seq_count:
                                    visited[new_state] = seq_count
                                    queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, seq_count))
                
                # Find maximum sequences for this start point
                for state, count in visited.items():
                    if count > max_sequences:
                        max_sequences = count

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Inna loves Dima, so she wants to go through his name as many times as possible as she moves through the table. For that, Inna acts as follows:

  initially, Inna chooses some cell of the table where letter "D" is written;  then Inna can move to some side-adjacent table cell that contains letter "I"; then from this cell she can go to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains the written letter "M"; then she can go to a side-adjacent cell that contains letter "A". Then Inna assumes that she has gone through her sweetheart's name;  Inna's next move can be going to one of the side-adjacent table cells that contains letter "D" and then walk on through name DIMA in the similar manner. Inna never skips a letter. So, from the letter "D" she always goes to the letter "I", from the letter "I" she always goes the to letter "M", from the letter "M" she always goes to the letter "A", and from the letter "A" she always goes to the letter "D". 

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^3). 

Then follow n lines that describe Inna and Dima's table. Each line contains m characters. Each character is one of the following four characters: "D", "I", "M", "A". 

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".


-----Output-----

If Inna cannot go through name DIMA once, print on a single line "Poor Dima!" without the quotes. If there is the infinite number of names DIMA Inna can go through, print "Poor Inna!" without the quotes. Otherwise print a single integer — the maximum number of times Inna can go through name DIMA.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 2
DI

Output
Poor Dima!

Input
2 2
MA
ID

Output
Poor Inna!

Input
5 5
DIMAD
DIMAI
DIMAM
DDMAA
AAMID

Output
4



-----Note-----

Notes to the samples:

In the first test sample, Inna cannot go through name DIMA a single time.

In the second test sample, Inna can go through the infinite number of words DIMA. For that, she should move in the clockwise direction starting from the lower right corner.

In the third test sample the best strategy is to start from the cell in the upper left corner of the table. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to form the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. Movement: from D→I→M→A→D→I→M→A→... (cyclically)
4. She can only move to side-adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
5. We need to find the maximum number of complete "DIMA" cycles she can make

## Key Observations

1. This is essentially a graph problem where each cell is a node and edges exist between adjacent cells with consecutive letters in "DIMA"
2. We need to find cycles in this graph
3. If there's a cycle that can be entered and exited, we might get infinite sequences
4. We need to track the maximum number of complete "DIMA" cycles from any starting position

## Approach

1. Build a graph where edges exist between adjacent cells with consecutive letters in "DIMA"
2. For each possible starting position with 'D', we need to find the longest path that follows D→I→M→A→D→I→M→A...
3. We can use DFS with cycle detection to find if infinite cycles exist
4. If infinite cycles exist, return "Poor Inna!"
5. If no valid path exists, return "Poor Dima!"
6. Otherwise, return the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles

## Algorithm Steps

1. Create a directed graph where edge exists from cell with letter X to cell with letter Y if Y is the next in "DIMA" and they are adjacent
2. For each cell with 'D', do DFS to find the maximum number of complete cycles
3. Keep track of visited states to detect cycles (state = (row, col, current_letter))
4. If we detect a cycle that allows us to restart, we have infinite possibility
5. Otherwise, return the maximum count

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Build the sequence: D->I->M->A->D->I->M->A...
    sequence = ["D", "I", "M", "A"]
    
    # Graph structure: from each position, where can we go?
    # We'll use DFS with memoization
    
    def get_next_letter(current_char):
        idx = sequence.index(current_char)
        if idx + 1 >= len(sequence):
            return None
        return sequence[idx + 1]
    
    def can_reach_and_count(start_row, start_col):
        # DFS to find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
        # We use state: (row, col, current_letter, steps)
        # But we need to detect cycles
        
        # Keep track of visited states to detect cycles
        # state: (row, col, current_letter) -> number of complete DIMA cycles so far
        # For cycle detection, if we revisit a state, we have a cycle
        
        visited = set()
        stack = []
        
        # Start with (row, col, letter) and count of completed DIMA cycles
        # We'll track the maximum count
        max_count = 0
        
        # DFS from the starting position
        def dfs(row, col, current_char, cycles):
            nonlocal max_count
            
            # Current state
            state = (row, col, current_char)
            
            if state in visited:
                # We found a cycle! We can go infinitely
                return float('inf')
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            next_char = get_next_letter(current_char)
            if next_char is None:
                # We've completed a full DIMA cycle
                cycles += 1
                # But we need to continue from D
                # After A, we go back to D
                # So we need to check if we can continue from A to D
                return cycles
            
            # Try all 4 directions
            for dr, dc in directions:
                new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
                
                if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m:
                    if grid[new_row][new_col] == next_char:
                        # We can move to this cell
                        new_cycles = dfs(new_row, new_col, next_char, cycles)
                        if new_cycles == float('inf'):
                            return float('inf')
                        max_count = max(max_count, new_cycles)
            
            return cycles
        
        # But we made a mistake above - we need to properly track the cycles
        # Let's rewrite with better state management
        
        # Actually, let's use a different approach: for each starting position,
        # we find the maximum number of complete DIMA cycles we can make
        
        # We'll do a BFS/DFS that tracks the path and counts completed cycles
        # We need to detect if we can create an infinite loop
        
        # Reset visited and do proper DFS
        visited = set()
        max_cycles_for_this_start = 0
        
        def dfs_with_count(row, col, current_char, completed_cycles):
            nonlocal max_cycles_for_this_start
            
            state = (row, col, current_char)
            
            if state in visited:
                # We've seen this state before, meaning we can create an infinite loop
                return float('inf')
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            next_char = get_next_letter(current_char)
            if next_char is None:
                # We just completed a DIMA cycle
                completed_cycles += 1
                # We need to continue from D to I
                # So we look for D to start next cycle
                # But we just reached A, now we need to go from A back to D
                # Search for D that is adjacent to current position
                next_d_positions = []
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
                    if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == 'D':
                        next_d_positions.append((new_row, new_col))
                
                if next_d_positions:
                    # From any of these D positions, we can restart the cycle
                    for d_row, d_col in next_d_positions:
                        # We would get another cycle from here
                        result = dfs_with_count(d_row, d_col, 'D', completed_cycles)
                        if result == float('inf'):
                            return float('inf')
                        max_cycles_for_this_start = max(max_cycles_for_this_start, result)
                return completed_cycles
            
            # Otherwise, we need to continue to next_char
            for dr, dc in directions:
                new_row, new_col = row + dr, col + dc
                if 0 <= new_row < n and 0 <= new_col < m and grid[new_row][new_col] == next_char:
                    result = dfs_with_count(new_row, new_col, next_char, completed_cycles)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return float('inf')
                    max_cycles_for_this_start = max(max_cycles_for_this_start, result)
            
            return completed_cycles
        
        # Try all starting D positions
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    visited = set()
                    result = dfs_with_count(i, j, 'D', 0)
                    if result == float('inf'):
                        return "Poor Inna!"
                    max_cycles_for_this_start = max(max_cycles_for_this_start, result)
        
        return max_cycles_for_this_start
    
    # But the above has issues with how we count cycles
    
    # Let me restart with a cleaner approach
    
    # We'll do: for each starting D position, use DFS to find if we can form infinite cycles
    # and what's the maximum finite cycles we can form
    
    # First, check if any infinite cycle exists
    # An infinite cycle exists if from some state (r, c, letter), we can reach the same state again
    
    def has_infinite_cycle():
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, letter, path):
            state = (r, c, letter)
            if state in visited:
                return True
            
            visited.add(state)
            
            next_letter = get_next_letter(letter)
            if next_letter is None:
                # Completed a cycle, now look for D to restart
                # Check if we can go from current position to any D
                return False
            
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    if dfs(nr, nc, next_letter, path + [next_letter]):
                        return True
            return False
        
        # Check if we can start from any D and eventually enter a cycle
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    if dfs(i, j, 'D', []):
                        return True
        return False
    
    # Check if there's any infinite cycle
    if has_infinite_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # If no infinite cycle, find maximum cycles from each starting D
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # We'll use a modified DFS that finds the maximum cycles
    def max_cycles_from_start(start_r, start_c):
        # Use memoization to avoid recomputation

                return memo[state]

                # So we look for D in adjacent cells
                next_d_count = 0

                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        next_d_count += 1

                        pass

                return 1

            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_char:
                    result = dfs(nr, nc, next_char)
                    best = max(best, result)
            
            memo[state] = best
            return best

        # Actually, each time we go D->I->M->A, we complete one cycle

        def dfs_cycle(r, c, current_char, cycles):

            if state in memo:
                return memo[state]
            
            next_char = get_next_letter(current_char)
            if next_char is None:
                # We've completed a cycle

                        next_d = 1  # We just found one D

                # This is getting very complex
                
                # Instead, let's just try a simpler approach:
                # We'll find if from any D position, we can reach another D position
                # But this doesn't solve the cycle counting
                
                # I think I need to simplify the problem
                
                pass
            
            # Instead, let's do a BFS that counts the maximum cycles we can make
            # But let's go back to the original approach with cycle detection
            
        # After reading the problem again, I think the key insight is:
        # This is a path-finding problem on a directed graph where we want to maximize
        # the number of complete "DIMA" cycles.
        
        # We can use state (r, c, letter) and use memoization to store the maximum cycles

        # But we already have a function to detect infinite cycles.
```

Actually, I see there's a logical error in my cycle detection. Let me fix this with a simpler and correct approach:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # The sequence: D -> I -> M -> A -> D -> I -> ...
    sequence = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A']
    
    # Detect if there's an infinite cycle
    def has_infinite_cycle():
        # We use DFS with state (row, col, current_letter)
        # If we visit the same state again, we have a cycle
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, letter):
            state = (r, c, letter)
            if state in visited:
                return True
            visited.add(state)
            
            # Next letter in the sequence
            idx = sequence.index(letter) if letter in sequence else -1
            next_letter = None
            if idx != -1 and idx + 1 < len(sequence):
                next_letter = sequence[idx + 1]
            
            if next_letter is None:
                # We're at A, and next should be D to restart
                # Look for any adjacent cell with D
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        # From this D, we can start a new cycle
                        # We need to see if from here we can eventually reach a state we've seen
                        # This would create a loop
                        if dfs(nr, nc, 'D'):
                            return True
                return False
            
            # Try to move to next_letter in adjacent cells
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    if dfs(nr, nc, next_letter):
                        return True
            return False
        
        # Check from every D cell
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    if dfs(i, j, 'D'):
                        return True
        return False
    
    if has_infinite_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # No infinite cycle, find maximum number of complete DIMA cycles
    max_cycles = 0
    
    # Use memoization for each state (r, c, letter)
    # For each starting D position
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                # DFS with memoization to find maximum cycles
                memo = {}
                
                def dfs(r, c, letter):
                    state = (r, c, letter)
                    if state in memo:
                        return memo[state]
                    
                    # Find next letter in sequence
                    try:
                        idx = sequence.index(letter)
                        next_letter = sequence[(idx + 1) % 4]
                    except:
                        next_letter = None
                    
                    # If next_letter is None, it means we are at A
                    if next_letter is None:
                        # We are at A, next should be D
                        # Look for adjacent D cells
                        next_d_positions = []
                        for dr, dc in directions:
                            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                                next_d_positions.append((nr, nc))
                        
                        if next_d_positions:
                            # From each D, we can start a new cycle
                            # Each such D can lead to more cycles
                            max_from_d = 0
                            for dr, dc in next_d_positions:
                                max_from_d = max(max_from_d, dfs(dr, dc, 'D'))
                            result = 1 + max_from_d  # 1 for this completed cycle
                        else:
                            result = 0
                    else:
                        # Move to next_letter
                        best = 0
                        for dr, dc in directions:
                            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                            if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                                result = dfs(nr, nc, next_letter)
                                best = max(best, result)
                        result = best
                    
                    memo[state] = result
                    return result
                
                cycles = dfs(i, j, 'D')
                max_cycles = max(max_cycles, cycles)
    
    if max_cycles == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_cycles)
```

from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # The sequence: D -> I -> M -> A -> D -> I -> ...
    sequence = ['D', 'I', 'M', 'A']
    
    # Detect if there's an infinite cycle
    def has_infinite_cycle():
        # We use DFS with state (row, col, current_letter)
        # If we visit the same state again, we have a cycle
        visited = set()
        
        def dfs(r, c, letter):
            state = (r, c, letter)
            if state in visited:
                return True
            visited.add(state)
            
            # Next letter in the sequence
            idx = sequence.index(letter) if letter in sequence else -1
            next_letter = None
            if idx != -1 and idx + 1 < len(sequence):
                next_letter = sequence[idx + 1]
            
            if next_letter is None:
                # We're at A, and next should be D to restart
                # Look for any adjacent cell with D
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == 'D':
                        # From this D, we can start a new cycle
                        # We need to see if from here we can eventually reach a state we've seen
                        # This would create a loop
                        if dfs(nr, nc, 'D'):
                            return True
                return False
            
            # Try to move to next_letter in adjacent cells
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    if dfs(nr, nc, next_letter):
                        return True
            return False
        
        # Check from every D cell
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(m):
                if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                    if dfs(i, j, 'D'):
                        return True
        return False
    
    if has_infinite_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # No infinite cycle, find maximum number of complete DIMA cycles
    max_cycles = 0

                    state = (r, c, letter)
                    if state in memo:
                        return memo[state]

                    except:

                        # Look for adjacent D cells

                                next_d_positions.append((nr, nc))

                            # Each such D can lead to more cycles
                            max_from_d = 0
                            for dr, dc in next_d_positions:
                                max_from_d = max(max_from_d, dfs(dr, dc, 'D'))
                            result = 1 + max_from_d  # 1 for this completed cycle
                        else:
                            result = 0
                    else:
                        # Move to next_letter

                    return result

Inna and Dima bought a table of size n × m in the shop. Each cell of the table contains a single letter: "D", "I", "M", "A".

Depending on the choice of the initial table cell, Inna can go through name DIMA either an infinite number of times or some positive finite number of times or she can't go through his name once. Help Inna find out what maximum number of times she can go through name DIMA.

Note that it is not guaranteed that the table contains at least one letter "D".

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. The movement follows a cycle: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
4. We need to find the maximum number of times she can go through "DIMA"
5. Possible outcomes:
   - "Poor Dima!" - can't go through DIMA once
   - "Poor Inna!" - can go infinitely many times
   - A number - finite maximum times

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a graph problem where each letter is a node and we have edges between adjacent cells with consecutive letters in the sequence
2. From any D, we can go to adjacent I, from I to adjacent M, from M to adjacent A, from A to adjacent D
3. The path forms cycles - if there's a cycle, we can go infinitely
4. If there's no path from any D to form DIMA once, return "Poor Dima!"
5. If there's a cycle, we can go infinitely, return "Poor Inna!"
6. Otherwise, we need to find the maximum path length

## Algorithm Approach

1. Use graph traversal (DFS/BFS) from each 'D' to find all possible paths
2. For each starting D, we want to find the longest path that follows D→I→M→A→D→I→M→A...
3. The key insight is that if we can reach a D again after going through D→I→M→A, we have a cycle
4. We can use a state to track visited positions to detect cycles

## Implementation Strategy

For each starting D:
- Use DFS to explore all paths
- Keep track of the current position and the number of complete DIMA cycles
- If we reach a D again after completing a cycle, we have an infinite path
- Otherwise, we record the maximum finite path length

However, there's a simpler approach:
- If we can find a cycle in the DIMA path graph, return "Poor Inna!"
- Otherwise, find the maximum path length

But the problem is that we need to check if we can form a cycle from any starting point.

Actually, we can model this as a directed graph and check for strongly connected components. If there's any cycle that can be traversed, we have infinite paths.

Better approach:
1. For each cell with D, perform DFS to find all possible paths
2. Keep track of visited states (position, cycle count)
3. If we revisit a state, we have a cycle → infinite
4. Otherwise, return the maximum path length

But we need to be careful about performance.

Alternative insight: We have a fixed cycle D→I→M→A→D. We can model this as a state machine.

Actually, let's think of it as a graph where we have edges:
- D → I (adjacent)
- I → M (adjacent) 
- M → A (adjacent)
- A → D (adjacent)

We need to find if there's any cycle in this graph that can be traversed.

The key insight is that if we can start from a D and eventually return to a D through the path D→I→M→A→D, then we have a cycle and infinite paths.

So:
1. Check if there's any path starting from D that can return to D via I→M→A→D
2. If such a cycle exists, return "Poor Inna!"
3. Otherwise, find the maximum path length from any D

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # Directions for side-adjacent cells
    directions = [(0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)]
    
    # For each D position, we'll try to find the maximum path length
    # But we need to detect cycles first
    
    # Check for cycles: if we can go from D to I to M to A to D, we have a cycle
    def has_cycle():
        # BFS/DFS to check if we can form a cycle
        visited = set()
        
        # For each D, we try to see if we can reach D again after going through I,M,A
        queue = deque()
        
        for i, j in d_positions:
            if (i, j) not in visited:
                # We'll use state (row, col, step) where step indicates which letter we're expecting
                # We're at D, next expect I
                state = (i, j, 'D')  # expect next to be I
                visited.add((i, j, 'D'))
                queue.append(state)
        
        while queue:
            r, c, expected = queue.popleft()
            
            # Try to move to next letter in sequence
            next_expected = expected
            if expected == 'D':
                next_expected = 'I'
            elif expected == 'I':
                next_expected = 'M'
            elif expected == 'M':
                next_expected = 'A'
            elif expected == 'A':
                next_expected = 'D'  # back to D, cycle found!
            
            # Look for adjacent cells with the required letter
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    if grid[nr][nc] == next_expected:
                        # Found next letter
                        new_state = (nr, nc, next_expected)
                        if (nr, nc, next_expected) not in visited:
                            visited.add((nr, nc, next_expected))
                            queue.append(new_state)
                        # If we just went from A to D, we have a cycle
                        if next_expected == 'D':
                            # We've completed a DIMA cycle and returned to D
                            # Check if the current state has a D that we can use to start
                            # Actually, the state (nr, nc, 'D') means we're at D again
                            # This means we have a cycle - infinite paths
                            return True
        
        return False
    
    # Check if there's any cycle
    if has_cycle():
        print("Poor Inna!")
        return
    
    # If no cycle, find maximum path length from each D
    max_length = 0
    
    for i, j in d_positions:
        # Use DFS to find path length from this D
        stack = [(i, j, 0)]  # (row, col, steps so far)
        # steps so far means how many complete DIMA sequences we've completed
        visited = set()
        visited.add((i, j, 0))
        
        while stack:
            r, c, steps = stack.pop()
            
            # Current letter at (r,c)
            current_letter = grid[r][c]
            
            # Next expected letter
            next_expected = None
            if current_letter == 'D':
                next_expected = 'I'
            elif current_letter == 'I':
                next_expected = 'M'
            elif current_letter == 'M':
                next_expected = 'A'
            elif current_letter == 'A':
                next_expected = 'D'
            
            # Try to move to adjacent cells with next_expected
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    if grid[nr][nc] == next_expected:
                        # We can move to this cell
                        new_steps = steps + 1
                        new_state = (nr, nc, new_steps)
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            stack.append(new_state)
                            max_length = max(max_length, new_steps)
    
    if max_length == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_length)

solve()
```

But in my current implementation, I'm not correctly detecting the cycle.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with letters D, I, M, A
2. Inna wants to go through the sequence "DIMA" as many times as possible
3. Movement is only to adjacent cells (up, down, left, right)
4. The path follows: D → I → M → A → D → I → M → A → ...
5. We need to find the maximum number of complete "DIMA" sequences Inna can go through

## Key Insights

1. This is a graph traversal problem where we need to find cycles
2. Since we follow a strict pattern D→I→M→A→D, we can think of this as a directed graph with edges between:
   - D to I
   - I to M  
   - M to A
   - A to D
3. If there's a cycle that can be traversed infinitely, we return "Poor Inna!"
4. If no path exists, we return "Poor Dima!"
5. Otherwise, we find the maximum path length

## Approach

1. Model this as a graph where each cell is a node and we have directed edges between cells that form the D→I→M→A→D pattern
2. Use BFS/DFS from each possible starting D cell to find paths
3. For each path, we need to track how many complete DIMA sequences we can form
4. Since the graph is small (1000×1000), we can use BFS with state (row, col, current_letter) to track progress
5. We need to detect cycles - if we can return to a state we've seen before, we have an infinite loop

## Algorithm Design

1. For each cell containing 'D', start a BFS that tracks the current position and the current phase (0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A)
2. When we reach a cell with the next required letter, we increment our count
3. Use a visited set to detect cycles - if we visit the same state (row, col, phase) twice, we have infinite loops
4. Track the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences found

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_count = 0
    
    # For each starting D position, do BFS to find maximum DIMA sequences
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # State: (row, col, current_phase)
        # phase: 0=D, 1=I, 2=M, 3=A
        # We want to count complete DIMA sequences (4 letters)
        
        # visited set to detect cycles
        visited = set()
        queue = deque()
        
        # Start from D at (start_r, start_c) with phase 0
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0))
        visited.add((start_r, start_c, 0))
        
        current_count = 0
        
        # We need to find the longest path without cycles
        # But we need to detect if we can form infinite loops
        
        while queue:
            r, c, phase = queue.popleft()
            
            # Determine what letter we need next based on phase
            if phase == 0:  # Need D
                next_needed = 'D'
                next_phase = 1
            elif phase == 1:  # Need I
                next_needed = 'I'
                next_phase = 2
            elif phase == 2:  # Need M
                next_needed = 'M'
                next_phase = 3
            elif phase == 3:  # Need A
                next_needed = 'A'
                next_phase = 0
            
            # Check if we can move to next letter
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m:
                    if grid[nr][nc] == next_needed:
                        # Found valid next move
                        new_phase = next_phase
                        new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                        
                        # If we've seen this state before, we have a cycle
                        if new_state in visited:
                            # This means we can loop infinitely
                            # But we need to detect this before we know the count
                            # Actually, we should check if we can form complete sequences
                            # Let's restart and check for cycles differently
                            # Instead, we'll do a different approach
                            pass
                        
                        # Add to queue to explore
                        if new_state not in visited:
                            visited.add(new_state)
                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase))
        
        # This approach is getting messy. Let's rethink.
        
    # Better approach: For each starting position, find the longest path to any cycle
    
    # Actually, let's use a different method: we'll simulate the path and detect cycles
    
    # For each starting D, we want to find maximum complete DIMA sequences
    # We can use BFS with state (r, c, phase) and track the number of complete cycles
    
    max_count = 0
    
    # Try all starting D positions
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # BFS from each start
        dist = {}  # (r, c, phase) -> max complete sequences reached (or -1 for cycle)
        queue = deque()
        
        # Initialize: start at D with phase 0, 0 sequences completed
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0, 0))  # (r, c, phase, sequences)
        dist[(start_r, start_c, 0)] = 0
        
        found_infinite = False
        current_max = 0
        
        while queue:
            r, c, phase, sequences = queue.popleft()
            
            # Check if we have a better count
            if sequences > current_max:
                current_max = sequences
                
            # Determine next letter needed
            next_letter = None
            if phase == 0:  # D -> I
                next_letter = 'I'
            elif phase == 1:  # I -> M
                next_letter = 'M'
            elif phase == 2:  # M -> A
                next_letter = 'A'
            elif phase == 3:  # A -> D
                next_letter = 'D'
            
            # If no next letter needed, we're at A, which should go to D
            # But we need to move
            if next_letter is not None:
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        new_phase = (phase + 1) % 4
                        
                        # Check if this state has been visited with same or better sequence count
                        # We need to detect cycles
                        
                        # If we've seen this (r, c, phase) state before, and we're at the same phase
                        # and we're about to go to a state we've seen before, we have a cycle
                        
                        new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                        new_sequences = sequences + 1 if new_phase == 0 else sequences
                        
                        # If we're at phase 0, we've completed one DIMA sequence
                        # But at phase 0, we're at D, which is the start of new DIMA
                        
                        # Actually, we want to count complete sequences
                        # When we go from A to D (phase 3 to 0), we complete one DIMA
                        
                        # So when we reach phase 0, we've completed one DIMA sequence
                        
                        if new_phase == 0:
                            new_sequences = sequences + 1
                        
                        # Check if we've seen this state before with same or higher sequence count
                        # But if we see the same state again, we have an infinite loop
                        
                        if new_state in dist:
                            # If we've seen this state with same or higher sequences, we have a cycle
                            if dist[new_state] >= new_sequences:
                                found_infinite = True
                                # We don't add to queue to avoid infinite growth
                                continue
                        else:
                            dist[new_state] = new_sequences
                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, new_sequences))
        
        # If we found an infinite loop, mark infinite
        if found_infinite:
            print("Poor Inna!")
            return
        
        # Otherwise, update max_count
        if current_max > max_count:
            max_count = current_max
    
    # If no sequence found, return poor Dima
    if max_count == 0:
        print("Poor Dima!")
    else:
        print(max_count)

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions: up, down, left, right
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]

                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    max_count = 0
    found_infinite = False
    
    # For each starting D position, we'll do BFS to see if we can get an infinite loop
    # and what's the maximum finite count

        # We'll use state (r, c, phase) where phase is 0,1,2,3
        # We want to track the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
        # and detect if we can have a cycle
        
        visited = {}  # (r, c, phase) -> max_sequences we can reach from that state
        queue = deque()
        
        # Start at (start_r, start_c) with phase 0, 0 sequences
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0, 0))
        visited[(start_r, start_c, 0)] = 0
        
        while queue:
            r, c, phase, sequences = queue.popleft()
            
            # Calculate next needed letter
            next_phase = (phase + 1) % 4
            next_letter = None
            if phase == 0:  # D->I
                next_letter = 'I'
            elif phase == 1:  # I->M
                next_letter = 'M'
            elif phase == 2:  # M->A
                next_letter = 'A'
            elif phase == 3:  # A->D
                next_letter = 'D'
            
            if next_letter is not None:
                for dr, dc in directions:
                    nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                    if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                        new_phase = next_phase
                        new_sequences = sequences
                        
                        # When we complete a DIMA cycle (from A to D), sequences increases
                        if new_phase == 0:
                            # We've completed one DIMA sequence
                            new_sequences += 1
                        
                        new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                        
                        # If we've seen this state before, check if we can form cycle
                        if new_state in visited:
                            # We see a cycle if the new_sequences is not greater than what we already had
                            # But if new_sequences is greater, we overwrote
                            if visited[new_state] < new_sequences:
                                visited[new_state] = new_sequences
                                queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, new_sequences))
                            # If visited[new_state] >= new_sequences, we have a cycle
                        else:
                            visited[new_state] = new_sequences
                            queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, new_sequences))
            
            # Check if we have a cycle: if we visit the same state with same phase but different sequences
            # Actually, we don't know if it's periodic

        # We need to check if any state can lead back to a previous state
        # But for now, let's just check if any state can form cycle
        
        # Find the maximum sequences from this start
        if visited:
            max_from_start = max(visited.values())
            if max_from_start > 0:
                max_count = max(max_count, max_from_start)
    
    # If we found any infinite cycle (cycle in the state space), then answer is infinite
    # How to detect infinite cycle? If we can return to a state with the same phase
    # but we can make more sequences, it means we can loop
    
    # Actually, this is getting too complex. Let me look for a simpler solution.
    
    # After research, I recall this is a classic cycle detection problem.
    # We can use the fact that we have a directed acyclic path that loops.
    
    # Simpler approach:
    # For each starting D, use BFS and record maximum sequences
    # If any path can form a cycle (return to same state), then infinite
    # Otherwise, find maximum sequences
    
    # I'll restart with a cleaner implementation
    
    max_count = 0
    infinite_found = False
    
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # State: (r, c, phase) -> best sequences from this state
        # Use BFS and detect cycles when we visit same state
        best = {}
        queue = deque()
        
        # Start with (start_r, start_c, 0) and 0 sequences
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0, 0))
        best[(start_r, start_c, 0)] = 0
        
        while queue:
            r, c, phase, sequences = queue.popleft()

            next_phase = (phase + 1) % 4
            next_letter = "I" if phase == 0 else "M" if phase == 1 else "A" if phase == 2 else "D"

                    if new_state in best:

                    if new_phase == 0:

                            # Actually, if we can improve on the best value, we might have a cycle
                            # But we don't know if it will loop
                            # Let's just mark that we have potential for infinite
                            pass

        # If any state has been visited with better sequences, it might be infinite

        # Since we're not detecting cycles properly, let me use a simpler known solution:

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(n):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Directions
    directions = [(-1, 0), (1, 0), (0, -1), (0, 1)]
    
    # Find all D positions
    d_positions = []
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(m):
            if grid[i][j] == 'D':
                d_positions.append((i, j))
    
    if not d_positions:
        print("Poor Dima!")
        return
    
    # For each starting D, find the maximum number of complete DIMA sequences
    # and detect if there's a cycle
    
    max_count = 0
    has_cycle = False
    
    for start_r, start_c in d_positions:
        # State: (r, c, phase)
        # visited to detect cycles
        visited = set()
        # best[r][c][phase] = max sequences from this state
        best = {}
        
        queue = deque()
        queue.append((start_r, start_c, 0, 0))
        visited.add((start_r, start_c, 0))
        best[(start_r, start_c, 0)] = 0
        
        while queue:
            r, c, phase, sequences = queue.popleft()
            
            # Next letter needed
            next_letter = "I" if phase == 0 else "M" if phase == 1 else "A" if phase == 2 else "D"
            
            for dr, dc in directions:
                nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
                if 0 <= nr < n and 0 <= nc < m and grid[nr][nc] == next_letter:
                    new_phase = (phase + 1) % 4
                    new_sequences = sequences
                    
                    # Complete a DIMA when we finish from A to D
                    if new_phase == 0:
                        new_sequences += 1
                    
                    new_state = (nr, nc, new_phase)
                    
                    # If we haven't seen this state, explore
                    if new_state not in visited:
                        visited.add(new_state)
                        best[new_state] = new_sequences
                        queue.append((nr, nc, new_phase, new_sequences))
                        
                    # If we have seen this state before, we might have a cycle
                    # But we only know current sequences
                    # If new_sequences > best[new_state], we can improve, so might have cycle
                    if new_state in best and new_sequences > best[new_state]:
                        # We can achieve more sequences, which might mean infinite loop
                        has_cycle = True
                        # We don't add to queue because we're detecting potential cycle
                        # But we continue to see if we can find better values
                        pass
        
        # After processing this start, check if we have a cycle
        # If any cycle is detected, mark has_cycle
        # Note: we set has_cycle in the loop, so if any start has cycle, we break
        
        if has_cycle:
            break
    
    if has_cycle:
        print("Poor Inna!")
    else:
        # Find the maximum sequences from all paths
        max_val = 0
        for state in best:
            if best[state] > max_val:
                max_val = best[state]
        if max_val == 0:
            print("Poor Dima!")
```
