Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 means working and 1 means resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours (1s) considering that the day wraps around
4. It's guaranteed there's at least one working hour (so not all 1s)

## Key Insight

Since days repeat endlessly, we need to consider:
1. Continuous rests within a single day (normal case)
2. Continuous rests that cross from the end of one day to the beginning of the next day

The maximum continuous rest could be:
- The longest sequence of consecutive 1s within one day, OR
- A sequence that starts at the end of a day and continues at the beginning of the next day

## Approach

1. First, find the maximum consecutive 1s in a single day (normal case)
2. Check if there's a wrap-around sequence: count how many 1s are at the end of the day and how many 1s are at the beginning of the day, and see if they can form a larger continuous sequence when connected

For the wrap-around case:
- Count consecutive 1s from the end of the array (right side)
- Count consecutive 1s from the beginning of the array (left side)
- The wrap-around sequence would be: (end consecutive 1s) + (beginning consecutive 1s) - 1 (since the connecting point is counted twice)

But we need to be careful - we only include wrap-around if there are 1s at both ends.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find maximum consecutive 1s in a single day (normal case)
2. Check if we can form a larger sequence by wrapping around
3. Return the maximum

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum consecutive 1s in a single day
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0

for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

# Check for wrap-around case
# Count consecutive 1s from the end of the day
end_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        end_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# Count consecutive 1s from the beginning of the day
begin_consecutive = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        begin_consecutive += 1
    else:
        break

# If there are 1s at both ends, we can form a wrap-around sequence
# The wrap-around sequence would be end_consecutive + begin_consecutive - 1
# (we subtract 1 because the connecting point is counted twice)
wrap_around = 0
if end_consecutive > 0 and begin_consecutive > 0:
    wrap_around = end_consecutive + begin_consecutive - 1
    # We need to check if this wrap-around sequence is valid
    # It's valid if the sequence from the end to the beginning can actually connect
    # which it does by the nature of wrapping

# The answer is the maximum of normal consecutive and wrap-around
result = max(max_consecutive, wrap_around)

# Special case: if the entire day is 0, but problem guarantees at least one working hour
# So we don't need to handle that case

print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

```python
# But we might have a sequence that spans across days? 
# From end of day to beginning of next day: positions 4,5,0,1 of next day
# But position 4:1, 5:1, then next day position 0:1, 1:1 -> so positions 5 and 0 are consecutive? 
# In the doubled array, position 5 is 1, position 6 is 1 (which is original position 0), so if position 5 and 6 are both 1, that's consecutive.

# But in the pattern [1,1,0,0,1,1], position 5 is 1, next day position 0 is 1, so in doubled array position 5 is 1 and position 6 is 1, so we have two consecutive 1's.

# We don't get more than 2.

# But how about if we have a pattern like [1,0,1,0,1,1,1]? 
# Within day: 3 at positions 5,6,7? No, only 5,6 are 1,1 -> 2 hours.
# In doubled array: we might get more.

# Actually, the doubled array approach will catch any sequence of consecutive 1's that might span across boundaries.

# However, we must be careful: if we have a sequence that spans more than n hours, is that valid?

# The problem says "maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests" and days go endlessly, so yes, it can span multiple days.

# But we need to check if our doubled array approach gives the correct result.

# Let's implement the doubled array approach:

# Steps:
# 1. Create doubled array of a
# 2. Find the maximum length of consecutive 1's in the doubled array
# 3. But we might have sequences that are longer than n, which is okay

# However, there's a catch: the sequence might span more than the day length, but that's fine.

# But wait: if we have a sequence that spans k hours across days, that's valid.

# However, in the example [1,1,0,0,1,1], the maximum within a day is 2, and in doubled array we find 2, so it's fine.

# But is there a case where the doubled array gives a sequence longer than what we should?

# Consider if we have [1,0,1,0,1] as in example 1.
# Double: [1,0,1,0,1,1,0,1,0,1]
# Consecutive 1's: 
# - position 0:1 (length 1)
# - position 2:1 (length 1)
# - position 4:1 (length 1)
# - position 5:1 (length 1) 
# - positions 5 and 6: position 5:1, position 6:0 (original position 1) -> so position 5 has 1, next is 0
# - position 7:1 (original position 2), etc.

# Actually, position 4:1, position 5:1 (original position 0) -> so at positions 4 and 5: values [1,1] -> length 2.

# Yes! So we get 2.

# So the algorithm is:
# 1. Create doubled array
# 2. Find the maximum consecutive 1's in the doubled array
# 3. Return that maximum

# But wait, in this doubled array, we might have sequences that are very long? 
# For example, if we have [1,1,1,...,1] with all 1's, but the problem guarantees at least one working hour.

# So we don't have all 1's.

# However, the doubled array might have a sequence that spans many hours, but that's valid.

# But is there a possibility that we get a sequence that is longer than n? Yes, but that's acceptable.

# However, let's test with example 3: [1,0,1,1,1,0,1]
# Within day: positions 2,3,4: 3 hours, so maximum is 3.
# In doubled array: we should also get 3.
# Positions: 2,3,4 are 1,1,1 -> length 3.

# Example 2: [0,1,0,1,1,0]
# Within day: positions 3,4: 2 hours.
# In doubled array: we have positions 3,4: 1,1 -> length 2.
# Also positions 5:0, then next day position 0:0, so no extra.
# And positions 1,2: position 1:1, position 2:0 -> not consecutive.
# So maximum is 2.

# But what about wraparound for example 2? 
# End of day: position 5 is 0, so no end rest.
# Start of day: position 0 is 0, so no start rest.
# So maximum is 2.

# This seems to work.

# However, there's a problem: what if the maximum sequence in the doubled array is longer than the maximum within a day, but it's actually not a valid rest period?

# Actually, any sequence of consecutive 1's in the doubled array represents a valid rest period that might span across days.

# For example, if we have [1,0,1,0,1], in doubled array we get a sequence of 2 at positions 4 and 5 (which are 1 and 1).

# So the code is:

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create doubled array
doubled = a + a

# Find maximum consecutive 1's in doubled array
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0

for i in range(2 * n):
    if doubled[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

print(max_consecutive)
```

Wait, but is this efficient? n up to 2*10^5, so 2*n is 4*10^5, which is acceptable.

But is there a case where we get more than the maximum within a day?

So it seems that we never get more than the maximum within a single day or the combination across days.

But actually, we can get more in the doubled array if the pattern allows.

Wait, what about [0,1,0,1]?
Within day: max 1
doubled = [0,1,0,1,0,1,0,1]
Consecutive: 
- positions 1:1 (length 1)
- positions 3:1 (length 1)
- positions 5:1 (length 1)
- positions 7:1 (length 1)
- at positions 1 and 3: not consecutive
- at positions 3 and 5: not consecutive (position 4 is 0)
So max is 1.

But in this case, the pattern has no two consecutive 1's, so it's correct.

I think the doubled array approach is correct.

Let me implement it.

However, there's a potential issue: what if we have a sequence that spans more than n hours? Is that allowed?

The problem says "continuous hours during which Polycarp rests", and since days repeat endlessly, yes, a rest period can span multiple days.

For example, if we have [1,0,1], the rest at hour 0 of day 1, and hour 2 of day 1, then in the next day, hour 0 of day 2, we have rest at hour 2 of day 1 and hour 0 of day 2.

So from hour 2 of day 1 to hour 0 of day 2: that's two hours.

In the doubled array, we see a sequence of length 2.

So it's valid.

Therefore, the solution is to create a doubled array and find the maximum consecutive 1's.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Create doubled array to check for wraparound sequences
doubled = a + a

# Find maximum consecutive 1's in the doubled array
max_consecutive = 0
current_consecutive = 0

for i in range(2 * n):
    if doubled[i] == 1:
        current_consecutive += 1
        max_consecutive = max(max_consecutive, current_consecutive)
    else:
        current_consecutive = 0

print(max_consecutive)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Each day in Berland consists of $n$ hours. Polycarp likes time management. That's why he has a fixed schedule for each day — it is a sequence $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ (each $a_i$ is either $0$ or $1$), where $a_i=0$ if Polycarp works during the $i$-th hour of the day and $a_i=1$ if Polycarp rests during the $i$-th hour of the day.

Days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.

What is the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests? It is guaranteed that there is at least one working hour in a day.


-----Input-----

The first line contains $n$ ($1 \le n \le 2\cdot10^5$) — number of hours per day.

The second line contains $n$ integer numbers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ ($0 \le a_i \le 1$), where $a_i=0$ if the $i$-th hour in a day is working and $a_i=1$ if the $i$-th hour is resting. It is guaranteed that $a_i=0$ for at least one $i$.


-----Output-----

Print the maximal number of continuous hours during which Polycarp rests. Remember that you should consider that days go one after another endlessly and Polycarp uses the same schedule for each day.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 0 1 0 1

Output
2

Input
6
0 1 0 1 1 0

Output
2

Input
7
1 0 1 1 1 0 1

Output
3

Input
3
0 0 0

Output
0



-----Note-----

In the first example, the maximal rest starts in last hour and goes to the first hour of the next day.

In the second example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $4$-th to the $5$-th hour.

In the third example, Polycarp has maximal rest from the $3$-rd to the $5$-th hour.

In the fourth example, Polycarp has no rest at all.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a daily schedule of n hours, where 0 = working, 1 = resting
2. Days repeat endlessly
3. We need to find the maximum number of continuous resting hours across all days
4. The key insight is that we might have a continuous rest period that crosses from the end of one day to the beginning of the next

## Key Observations

1. First, we need to find the maximum continuous resting hours within a single day.
2. We also need to check if there's a longer resting period that crosses the day boundary (from end of one day to start of next).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find maximum continuous rest within a single day
- Scan through the array and count consecutive 1s
- Keep track of the maximum

### Step 2: Check for cross-day resting periods
- Count consecutive 1s that start from the end of the array and continue from the beginning
- For example, if last few hours are 1 and first few hours are 1, we might have a longer continuous period

### Step 3: Special case handling
- If there are no resting hours (all 0s), return 0
- The problem guarantees at least one working hour, so there will be at least one 0

## Implementation Strategy

1. Calculate the maximum continuous rest within the day
2. For cross-day periods, we need to check the suffix of 1s and prefix of 1s
3. The cross-day rest would be (suffix of 1s at end) + (prefix of 1s at beginning)

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: `[1, 0, 1, 0, 1]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s is 1 (single 1s)
- End of day: last hour is 1, so suffix of 1s is 1
- Beginning of day: first hour is 1, so prefix of 1s is 1
- Cross-day: 1 + 1 = 2 ✓

Example 2: `[0, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s is 2 (positions 3-4)
- Cross-day: suffix of 1s is 0 (ends with 0), prefix of 1s is 0 (starts with 0)
- So max is 2 ✓

Example 3: `[1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 1]`
- Within day: max consecutive 1s is 3 (positions 3-5)
- Cross-day: suffix of 1s is 1 (ends with 1), prefix of 1s is 1 (starts with 1)
- Cross-day: 1 + 1 = 2, which is less than 3
- So max is 3 ✓

## Code Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find maximum continuous rest within a single day
max_in_day = 0
current = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        current += 1
        max_in_day = max(max_in_day, current)
    else:
        current = 0

# Check for cross-day rest (from end of one day to start of next)
# Count consecutive 1s from the end
suffix_ones = 0
for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
    if a[i] == 1:
        suffix_ones += 1
    else:
        break

# Count consecutive 1s from the beginning
prefix_ones = 0
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == 1:
        prefix_ones += 1
    else:
        break

# Cross-day rest would be suffix_ones + prefix_ones
cross_day_rest = suffix_ones + prefix_ones

# The answer is the maximum of within-day rest and cross-day rest
result = max(max_in_day, cross_day_rest)

print(result)
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the cross-day rest. The cross-day rest is actually the sum of suffix and prefix, but this gives the total length of the cross-day rest period. The cross-day rest should be at least the sum of the suffix and prefix, but we need to make sure this doesn't create an invalid situation.

Actually, my approach is correct. Let me verify with example 1:
- Array: [1, 0, 1, 0, 1]
- max_in_day: max consecutive 1s within day = 1
- suffix_ones: count from end - last element is 1, so it's 1
- prefix_ones: first element is 1, so it's 1
- cross_day_rest = 1 + 1 = 2
- result = max(1, 2) = 2 ✓

This matches. The code looks correct.

```python
    else:
```
